THE NUMBERS OF ALGEBRA 

In arithmetic you first learned about the whole numbers or integers and how to use them; next you learned about the common fractions and how to use them; then about decimals or decimal fractions, for decimals are only a new way to write certain common fractions. 

In algebra, you have learned about positive and negative numbers. Such numbers were introduced in order to express opposites and in order that we may always subtract one number from another. They become necessary especially when solving equations.

Recently you have learned about numbers that are needed to express the square roots of those positive integers that are not squares, as √2 or √5. If the number under the radical is not a square, then the indicated square root is an irrational number; it cannot be expressed exactly as an integer or as a common fraction. These numbers are needed in order that we may solve certain quadratic equations. 

In this chapter, another kind of number becomes necessary in order that we may solve certain quadratics which we have avoided so far. These numbers, you will find, enable us to express the square root of a negative number. They are called imaginary numbers, although they are no more “imaginary" than other numbers.

The sum of an imaginary number and a real number is a complex number. Complex numbers are used in the study of electricity and some of its applications.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN ELECTRICAL ENGINEERING 

The unpretentious box at the left is a fault detector developed in the General Electric General Engineering and Consulting Laboratory to spot foreign particles or holes as small as .003 in. in paper, cloth or any flexible sheet material, when the material is run through the box at speeds as high as 450 feet per minute.

Electrical engineers are called upon to design, construct and install electrical devices for almost all conceivable purposes, large and small. They are trained first in engineering schools which require second course algebra for admission, and at least two years of pure mathematics in college.

Conjugate Complex Numbers 

183 The numbers 3 + 2i and 3 - 2i are called conjugate complex numbers.

The conjugate of xiy is x + iy. 

The sum of two conjugate complex numbers and also the product of them is real.

Classification of Numbers 

The numbers of arithmetic are the integers and fractions. 

Integers are used for counting objects.

Two integers can always be added or multiplied. 

One integer can be subtracted from another only when it is less than the other; one integer can be divided by another only if it contains the other exactly. 

Fractions cannot be used for counting.

Two integers or fractions can always be added, multiplied, or divided now. Subtraction is still impossible unless the minuend is greater than the subtrahend. 

Signed numbers include the positive and negative integers and fractions. It is always possible to add, subtract, multiply, and divide two positive and negative numbers. 

Rational numbers include the positive and negative integers and fractions.

Irrational numbers include the square roots and other roots of positive numbers; as √2 and √5. Two of them can always be added, subtracted, multiplied, and divided, and by means of them any root of a positive number can be expressed. 

Imaginary numbers are the square roots of negative numbers, as √-2, √ 4. Two of them can always be added, subtracted, multiplied, or divided, raised to any power.

Summary. The numbers of algebra include: 

A. The real numbers. 

1. The rational numbers; namely, the positive integers and fractions. 

2. The irrational numbers; namely, the roots of positive integers and some other numbers. 

B. The imaginary numbers. 

1. The pure imaginaries; namely, the even roots of negative integers. 

2. The complex numbers, consisting of the sum of a real number and a pure imaginary.

189 The kind of roots in a particular equation depends on the value of b² - 4ac. For this reason, b² - 4ac is called the discriminant of the quadratic. Rules. 

1. If b2-4ac is positive, the roots are real and unequal. (a) They are rational when b² — 4ac is a square number. (b) They are irrational when b2-4ac is not a square. 

2. If b2-4ac = 0, the roots are real and equal. 

3. If b2-4ac is negative, the roots are imaginary or complex numbers.

Quadratic Functions 

195 You know that the value of x2 depends on the value of x. 

For each value of x there is a definite value of x². 

We say x2 is a function of x; x is a variable. 

Similarly, 2x2 is a function of x; also x2 and 3x² + 2x − 4. 

Many laws of geometry and science involve quadratic functions of some variable, so that knowledge of such functions and their graphs is important.

SYSTEMS OF QUADRATICS 

In Chapter 6 you learned how to draw the graph of an equation of the first degree having two unknowns or variables; for example, an equation like 2x + 3y = 12.

You learned that the graph of every equation of this kind is a straight line. The graphs of such equations were introduced to prepare for and aid in the study of sets or pairs of such equations, as, 3x+2y= 14 { 2 5x-3y = - In this chapter you will study the graphs of equations of the second degree having two unknowns, such as: x² + y² = 25 or 4x²+9y² = 36

These graphs will prove interesting to you and will help you to solve systems of two quadratic equations having two unknowns, and to solve problems by them. 

You will be surprised that certain familiar curves can be represented by quadratic equations having two unknowns. 

This chapter actually contains selected easy topics from a branch of mathematics called analytic geometry that was studied only in college prior to recent years. You will not find it difficult at all to master these topics, and it may lead you to desire to study analytic geometry if you go to college. 

Trigonometry and analytic geometry are, with algebra, the principal doorways to higher mathematics. 

The curves studied in this chapter are called conic sections because they can be obtained as intersections of a cone by a plane.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN MODERN RESEARCH 

WESTINGHOUSE MANUFACTURING COMPANY 

The picture above shows a research engineer of the Westinghouse Laboratories measuring the cone in the reflected image of a flame in his study of flame velocities aimed at improvement of the design of jet engines for highspeed aircraft. His measurements will help him to calculate the speed at which fuel energy is converted into heat-knowledge that is vital in efficient designing of aviation gas turbine engines.

The training and the work of such engineers includes the study of mathematics and the physical sciences.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN DEFENSE 

GENERAL ELECTRIC COMPANY 

V-2 rockets have been launched at the New Mexico Ordnance Proving Grounds to study and develop such missiles and counter-measures against them. General Electric Company scientists and engineers, using an advanced-mathematics computing machine, obtained in ten days results that would have required the efforts of a single mathematician for three months.

SYSTEMS OF QUADRATIC EQUATIONS 

Two Unknowns If you were to draw the graphs of the functions x² and x²-6x + 9, or (x − 3)² by using the equations = x² and y = (x − 3)² you would obtain the adjoining graphs. Notice that the graph of (x-3)2 is exactly the same as that of x², but moved 3 units to the right.

Similarly, the graph of (x+3)² or [x-(-3)]² X' would be like them, but moved - 3 units, or 3 units to the left. 2 ہم 3-2-1-0. 1-2 3+ 4+ 5 These facts are true of the graphs of all functions of one unknown. They are expressed by the following rule.

Rule. If xa is substituted for x in any equation involving only x and y, the graph of the resulting equation is the same as that of the given equation but moved "a" units to the right or left, according as "a" is positive or negative.

The corresponding rule is true if yb is substituted for y.

Thus: The graph of the equation y 5 = x² is the same as that of y = x² but moved up 5 units. 

Similarly, the graph of y+4= (x3)2 is the same as that of y = x², moved 3 units to the right and 4 units down. 201

The graph of every equation of the form x² + y² = r² is a circle with center at the origin and radius r. This fact is proved in analytic geometry. 

On the next page the converse of this statement is proved, namely, the equation of the circle with center at the origin and radius r is x² + y² = r². 

The converse of a statement is not always true. In this case it is, as proved on page 203.

✩✩ Parabolas 

One definition of the parabola follows. 

Let F be a point at distance p on the X-axis; let DD' be a line parallel to the Y-axis, p units to the left of it; let P be any point such that PF equals the distance from P to DD'. X'

Then the points P form a parab- ola of the form bx y². In fact, the equation of this parabola is 

4px = y² or y² = 4px.

All this is taught in analytic geometry. 207 P/F D' Y' 

F is the focus of the parabola; DD' is its directrix.

The path of any projectile thrown or shot upward is a parabola.

Headlights on automobiles have parabolic reflectors. The light is at the focus. The result is that the rays of light are thrown forward parallel to the axis of the parabola.

Quadratic-Linear System 

Since the graph of a quadratic equation having two unknowns is a circle, parabola, ellipse, or hyperbola, and the graph of a linear equation is a straight line, a system consisting of one quadratic and a linear equation will give a figure like one of the following. * * * In general, a straight line crosses one of the four "quadratic" curves in two and only two points, so that the system will have two and only two real solutions. These solutions are the coordinates of point A and of point B. 

But, the straight line may not cut the curve at all. Then we have two imaginary solutions. Or, it may just touch the curve; then we have two solutions that are exactly alike. 

Thus: Draw a straight line tangent to a circle, and also draw one that fails to touch a circle.

Draw similar figures for a straight line tangent to an ellipse and failing to cut the ellipse.

Problems 1. The difference of two numbers is 7 and the sum of their squares is 109. What are the numbers? 

2. The difference of two numbers is 4 and the difference of their squares is 80. What are the numbers? 

Note: You will get only one common solution in this example because the system is reducible; that is, one equation contains the other. 

3. Find two numbers whose product is 35 and the sum of whose squares is 74. 

4. The perimeter of a rectangular picture frame is 70 inches; the area of it is 286 square inches. What are the dimensions of the frame? 

5. The length of the diagonal of a rectangular lot is 13 rods; the area of the lot is 60 square rods. What are the length and the width of the lot? 

6. The total area of two adjoining square pieces of ground is 

625 square yards. The side of one is 5 yards more than the side of the other. What is the length of a side of each piece? 

7. The length of the altitude of a triangle is 6 feet more than that of its base. If the area of the triangle is 140 square feet, what is the length of its base and altitude? 

8. Find two numbers such that the square of the first increased by twice the square of the second is 7, and the square of the second decreased by twice the square of the first is - 4. 

9. Find two numbers such that the sum of the square of the first and four times the square of the second is 25, and the first increased by twice the second is 7. 

10. What real numbers are there such that the sum of their squares is 37 and their product is 6? 

11. The ratio of two numbers is 1:3. Find the numbers: 

(a) If the sum of their squares is 40. Suggestion. 1 3 (b) If the difference of their squares is 72. (c) If 5 times the square of the smaller is 14 more than the larger.

Problems 219 12. A farmer has 100 rods of fence with which he wishes to inclose a field that will contain 2.5 acres of ground. How long and how wide may he make the field? 

Suggestion. One acre 160 square rods. 13. What must the length and width of a field be to have an area of 16 acres, if the ratio of the length to the width is to be 5:2? 

14. The difference of the squares of the ones' and tens' digits of a number is 24. If the digits be reversed, the new number is 

18 more than the original number. What is the number? 

15. The sum of a fraction and its reciprocal is 13. The sum of the squares of its numerator and denominator is 13. What is the fraction? 

16. The area of a trapezoid is 72 square inches. The square of the lower base is 4 times the square of the upper base. The altitude equals the sum of the bases. What are the lengths of the bases and altitude? 

17. The average rate of an airplane on a trip of 360 miles was four thirds its rate when returning the same distance. It required one half hour longer for the return trip than for the outbound trip. 

What was its rate in each direction? 

18. If a clothing merchant can get a discount of 20% from the asking price of a certain suit of clothes, he will be able to buy 2 more suits for $120 than he can at the original price. What is the asking price and how many suits can he buy at that price? 

19. If the wholesale price of a scratch pad is cut 2¢ a pad, a school supplies dealer will be able to get 40 more pads for $9.60 than he can at the original price. What was the original price? 

20. A rectangular field contains 5 acres. If its width were decreased by 4 rods, its area would be decreased by one acre. What are its length and width in rods? 

21. The difference of the rates of two trains is 20 miles an hour. 

One takes one hour less for a trip of 200 miles than the other requires for a trip of 150 miles. What is the rate of each? 

22. The total area of two adjoining square pieces of ground, fronting on a road, is 50 acres. The outside boundary is 400 rods. 

What are the lengths of the sides of the squares?

EXPONENTS-LOGARITHMS 

By this time you are quite familiar with positive integral exponents and how to multiply or divide powers of the same base.

Early mathematicians naturally associated x2 with the area of a square because the area of a square with side x is xxx. That is the reason why x2 was and is read x-square.

Similarly, x3 was associated with the volume of a cube having edges x, and was read x-cube. 

Mathematicians like to generalize. Having used positive integers as exponents, it was very natural presently to wonder what meaning should be given to an expression like x, x, x2, xº. No obvious geometric illustrations for these are apparent.

In this chapter you will learn what meanings these symbols have and how to use them.

Possibly more important was the introduction of certain special exponents called logarithms. Having found that multiplication of powers of a base was done by adding the exponents, some mathematicians of the early seventeenth century got the idea that they could make multiplication of numbers easy by expressing these numbers as powers of some base, since then they could replace multiplication of the numbers by adding their exponents.

The result of their work was the development of logarithms. 

This proved to be a far-reaching, important means of simplifying computation. You will learn about logarithms in the next chapter.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN MOLECULAR RESEARCH 

A Westinghouse research physicist, using apparatus he developed to analyze samples of complex chemical substances. Infrared rays, made to pass through the sample, strike sensitive receivers which measure their intensity. Analysis of the absorption pattern enables the operator to tell what compounds are in the substance.

The development of such apparatus is an outgrowth of the training in science and mathematics of men like the inventor of this equipment.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN CHEMICAL ENGINEERING 

The picture above is a close-up of part of apparatus used in the Experiment Station of E. I. du Pont de Nemours Company to determine the kinds and numbers of atoms in molecules of such materials as proteins, cellulose and rubber. The substance, in liquid form, is placed in the cell near the center of the picture. It is then revolved at rates as high as 60,000 times a minute. Just as a stone revolved at the end of a string is thrown away from the center by centrifugal force, so the heavier atoms are thrown to the far end of the cell and the lighter ones remain nearer the center of revolution. 

This machine is an example of apparatus used by a chemical engineer. He is called on to design and construct the machines to accomplish what laboratory chemists specify.

EXPONENTS-RADICALS 

The first step in this chapter will be to learn the meaning and use of the exponents and §. 

The Exponent 

Recall that 92 means 9 × 9, or that 9 is used as a factor two times. 

9 cannot mean that 9 is to be used as a factor times. 

Nothing can be done one half times. 

Thus: You cannot look out of a window one half times. Either you look out of the window, or you do not.

To arrive at a meaning for 9, we assume that it acts like positive integral powers of a number; that is, we assume that 9 × 9 = 91+1, or 9. Then 

9 must be √9 or 3.

OTHER ROOTS 

The fourth root of a number is that number whose fourth power equals the given number. (-2)4 = 16 4: 

(2i) = 16 

Thus: The fourth roots of + 16 are 2, 2, 2i, and - 2i because: 24 = 16 (-2) = 16 

Remember. Every positive number has four fourth roots, of which two are real and two are imaginary. Of the real roots, one is positive and one is negative. The positive one is the principal root; it is indicated by or by ( )ª. 

The fourth roots of a negative number are imaginary.

In general: Every number has n nth roots, of which some are real and some imaginary or complex numbers. 

The principal nth root of k is indicated by either V or by k; k is the radicand; n is the index of the root. 

When n is even, the principal root is positive if k is positive, and imaginary if k is negative.

When n is odd, the principal root is positive if k is positive, and negative if k is negative. 

Thus: √- 64 = 2i; √– 32 = √(− 2)5 - = -

Other Kinds of Exponents 

Negative, fractional, and zero exponents as well as positive integral exponents are used. x-³ cannot mean x used as a factor minus three times because you cannot do anything minus three times. 

Similarly, x cannot mean that x is used as a factor times.

FRACTIONAL EXPONENTS

★ Radicals Note. Pages 234 to 243 are included for schools that require study of radicals. (See Teacher's Manual.)

Indicated roots of all kinds are, for convenience, called radicals. When the root cannot be expressed as an integer or common fraction, the number is an irrational number. 

Often such a root of a positive number is called a surd. 

This section will consider the methods of simplifying and computing with cube and higher roots and give further practice in the solution of radical equations.

Recall that the number under a radical sign or that appears with a fractional exponent is the radicand. 

The index of a radical is the number that indicates the root of the radicand or it is the denominator of a fractional exponent, as 3, in the case of x³. 

Use of and computing with radicals is based on: 

(a) The definition: Vxm = xn 

(b) Use of fractional exponents in accordance with the laws of exponents. In particular: (1) (Vx)” = (x)n n = xn or x xor (2) Vxy = (xy) = (x) (yn) .. 

Vxy = x. Vy (3) 1815 ​y 218 1 xn yn The last two special rules may be used directly or fractional exponents may be used, as they were in deriving these special rules. Thus:

Or 

16x³y (√16)(√x³)(Vy¹), or 2x²y √16x³y¹ = (24x³y4)‡ 24x¤y, or 2x²y

LOGARITHMS 

Logarithms are exponents used to simplify certain computations with arithmetical numbers. 

Their use can be illustrated by some computations with numbers that are powers of the base 2. 1024 X 322

SOLUTION. 

1213 1. √512 × √1024 = (29) × (210) 2. 3. 23 X 25 = 28, or 256 The exponents used in these examples are logarithms. 

27 128. Then 7 is the logarithm of 128 to the base 2. For general computing, numbers are expressed as powers of the base 10. Thus: 100 102. Therefore 2 is the logarithm of 100 to the base 10. 243

History and Meaning of Logarithms 

A Scotch mathematician, John Napier (1550-1617), was the first to publish ideas about logarithms. He did not use 

10 or any ordinary number as the base of his logarithms. 

Briggs (1556-1630), a friend of Napier's, was greatly impressed by his ideas and suggested to him the advantage of using 10 as the base. By 1617, Briggs had computed the logarithms of all integers from 1 to 1000, and, seven years later, he published the logarithms of the integers from 1 to 20,000, and from 90,000 to 100,000. These logarithms are called the Briggs logarithms or common logarithms. 

Two Dutch mathematicians, De Decker and Vlacq, computed the logarithms of integers from 20,000 to 90,000. Definition.

If n = ba, then a = log, n. 

Thus: log4 64 = x. .. 64 = 4x 

x = 3, for 43 = 64. 2. 

y = 62, or 36. 

81 z4 or z = 3 since 34 81. logo y = log, 81 = 4.

Rules for Using Logarithms 245 When logarithms are used, they are logarithms to the same base. Since they are exponents: 

Rule 1. The logarithm of the product of two numbers is the sum of the logarithms of the numbers. 

That is: Thus: PROOF. 1. 2. loga MN = loga M + loga N log2 646; log2 128 = 7 .. log2 (64 × 128) = 6 + 7, or 13. 

Let M = a* or x loga M, and let N = a" or y = log. N.

MN ax+y :. .. loga MN = loga M + loga N loga MN = x + y Thus: *PROOF. 1. 2. 3. .. 3. 4.

Rule 2. The logarithm of a power of a number is the exponent times the logarithm of the number. 

That is: loga Mn = n loga M log2 643 = 3 log2 64 = 3 × 6, or 18. 

Let M = a* or x = loga M. .. Mn (ax)n loga M = nx or log. Mn = anx = n log. M

Rules for Using Logarithms

Rule 3. The logarithm of the quotient of two numbers is the difference of the logarithms of the numbers. That is: log. (M÷N) = log. M - loga N. Thus: log2 256 = 8; log₂ 16 = 4 256

Common Logarithms 247 Most numbers are not exact powers of 10. Therefore the logarithms of most numbers to base 10 are approximate mixed numbers. 

Thus: 274: = 102.4378

The integral part of a logarithm is its characteristic; as 

2; the decimal part is its mantissa; as .4378. 

(a) RULE FOR FINDING THE CHARACTERISTIC OF A LOG- ARITHM OF A NUMBER GREATER THAN 1. 

274 102.4378. .. .. 

10 × 274 

10¹× 102.4378 103.4378. .. log 274 = 2.4378 log 2740 = 3.4378 

10 × 2740 = 101 × 103.4378 = 104.4378. .. log 27400 = 4.4378

The characteristic of log 2740 is 3; of log 27400 is 4. 

Rule. The characteristic of the common logarithm of a number greater than 1 is one less than the number of digits in the number to the left of the decimal point. 

(b) RULE FOR FINDING THE CHARACTERISTIC OF THE LOGARITHM OF A NUMBER LESS THAN 1. 

.274 2.74 2.74 :. log .274 

100.4378 or log 2.74 0.4378 10 100.4378 10¹ 

100.4378-1 10-.5622 .5622, a negative number. However, it 

is customary to write 0.4378 1. The mantissa still is +.4378 and the characteristic is 1. Similarly: 

.0274 = 2.74 ÷ 100 · 100.4378-2 .00274 Therefore: 

2.74 ÷ 1000 

100.4378 ÷ 103 100.4378-3 .4378 1 

log .274 log .0274 = .4378 - 2 log .00274.4378 - 3 

Rule. The characteristic of the common logarithm of a positive number less than 1 is negative; the absolute value of it is one more than the number of zeros between the decimal point and the first significant digit of the number.

Finding the Characteristic 

A negative number cannot be expressed as a power of 10; therefore a negative number does not have a common logarithm. See the graph on page 249.

The rules given on page 247 apply only to common logarithms; that is the reason why common logarithms are so convenient.

Remember. When the logarithm of a number is a negative number, we use the following expressions: 

If the logarithm is .3275 1, we write 9.3275 or we may use 19.3275 

If the logarithm is .3275 – 2, we write 8.3275 

10; 

20, etc. 10; 

or we may use 18.3275 - 20, etc. 

In general: Decide on the characteristic; if it is - 1, write 9.. 10; if it is 2, write 8. - . — 

. 

10; etc. 

Frequently the "-10" is omitted, so that for 9.3275 - 10 we may write 9.3275; for 8.3275 – 10, we may write 8.3275. 

This is safe because a characteristic like 7, 8, or 9, without an understood 10, would indicate a very large number. When in doubt, write the 

10.

* Graph of y = log x 

249 The following table consists of a few values of x and their logarithms. 

When x = 1 .3 .6 1.0 1.8 3.2 5.6 10.00 then y = log x = .50 

.25 0.75 

0.00 0.25 0.50 1.00 +8 Y=log X .4 -0· 1. 2 -3- -4- 5 7 -9 10 This graph clearly indicates the following facts: (a) A negative number does not have a common logarithm. (b) Numbers between 0 and 1 have negative logarithms. (c) When a number increases, its logarithm (y) increases, but the increase is not proportional to the increase of the number, because the graph is not a straight line. 

Thus: When x increases from 3 to 4, log, increases .14. 

When x increases from 4 to 5, log, increases .10. 

When x increases from 5 to 6, log increases .08.

But, if two numbers are close together (like x = 5.0 and x = 5.2), the graph between them is approximately a straight line, and the increase in the logarithm is approximately proportional to the increase in x.

This fact is used when we find the logarithm of a number that is between two numbers whose logarithms are known, or are given in a table.

Mantissa of Logarithm 

Observe the following logarithms: log .2759.4393 log 2.75 0.4393 log 27.5 1.4393 log 275 2.4393 

Each of these numbers has the significant digits 275. The logarithms all have the mantissa .4393. 

Remember. The logarithms of all numbers that have the same significant digits have the same mantissa. 

The mantissas are in a "table of logarithms" such as that on pages 356 and 357. In this table, the mantissas are correct to four decimal places. Only the mantissas need be printed, since the characteristics of logarithms are obtained by inspection.

Finding the Number 

Next you must learn how to find the number when its logarithm is given. 

EXAMPLE 1. Find x if log x = 2.9196.

SOLUTION. 

1. In the table on pages 356 to 357, try to find the mantissa 9196. 

2. It appears opposite 83 and below 1. Hence 9196 must be the mantissa of log 831. 

3. Since the characteristic is 2, the number must have three digits before the decimal point. .. x = 831.

The number corresponding to a given logarithm is called its antilogarithm.

Often you must interpolate to find the number. 

EXAMPLE 2. Find the antilogarithm of 9.5280 - 10.

* Computing with a Slide Rule 

A simple slide rule is a device for multiplying and dividing and related kinds of computing. It is made possible by logarithms. It consists of two or more logarithmic scales so arranged that addition and subtraction of logarithms can be done mechanically.

The scale on an ordinary ruler is a uniform scale because all the units of one kind on it are of the same length. Such is the scale U below. Its length is used as the unit on it and on the scale L. 8 ༷ 

7 8 8 ➢ ཋ ཋ ཋ 8 བླ ུ གག༷ ༞ ༅ ཤྩ €ྣ ཀྵུ 1.00 1.5 2 2.5 3.5 4 4.5 5 5.5

L is a logarithmic scale. It is constructed as follows: log 1 = 0. log 10 = 1. log 2 = .30 

.. 

1 is placed on L below 0 of U. 

10 is placed on L below 1 of U. 

2 is placed on L below .30 of U.

The logarithm of each number on L is the number above it on U. 

Thus: log 4.5 is about .65.

The number at each point on U is the distance of the point from the left end of U and L. 

Thus: The distance of 4.5 of L and .65 of U from the left ends of the scales is .65, or log 4.5.

Logically the points of L should be marked log 1, log 1.5, log 2, log 2.5, etc. For convenience they are marked only by the numbers, the abbreviation "log" being omitted. 

Notice that 6 on L is not twice as far as 3 is from the left end of L; rather the distance of 6 is to the distance of 3 as log 6 is to log 3; that is, as .78 is to .48.

For this reason, the distance between consecutive integers (and other units) on L decreases as the integers increase. The logarithmic scale is nonuniform. ΟΙ

*Using a Slide Rule 

A simple slide rule has on it four logarithmic scales, usually marked A, B, C, D. 

At the right on this page, are scales C and D, placed in position for multiplying 1.55 by any number from 1 to almost 6.5.

On a 10" slide rule, the parts are divided into more subdivisions than in our figure.

In our figure, a number between 1 and 4 can be read correct to the nearest .05 and must be approximated beyond that, as 1.654. Between 

4 and 10, a number can be read correct to the nearest .1, but must be approximated for more digits, as 5.65. 

(a) TO MULTIPLY BY USING A SLIDE RULE EXAMPLE 1. Find 1.55 x 3.7. 

SOLUTION. 

1. 2. 3. 

Above 1.55 of D, place 1 of C. 

Below 3.7 of C is 5.7 on D. :. 1.55 x 3.7 = 5.7 

EXPLANATION. log (1.55 × 3.7) = log 1.55 + log 3.7

Our solution gives us the sum of these logarithms. 

It is marked 5.7 instead of log 5.7 because each point of L is marked by its antilogarithm, as explained on page 260. CHECK. 

1.55 x 3.7 = 5.735, or 5.7

EXAMPLE 2. Find 155 X 48.

NUMERICAL TRIGONOMETRY 

Some numerical trigonometry is part of recommended first courses in algebra. However, since it is uncertain how much of it has been studied by any class, the following chapter includes the parts of trigonometry that belong in the first course and, next, the parts that belong in the second course in algebra.

In the simplest problems of mathematics, any quantity to be measured can usually be handled or paced off and therefore can be measured directly. In many problems, however, the quantity to be measured cannot be reached, so that the measurements must be made indirectly, as distances across water, the height of an airplane, the distance to and the size of one of the heavenly bodies. In all such instances, the measurements desired are obtained by computation based on measurements of distances and angles that can be reached. Trigonometry is the special means employed in making these computations. 

Indirect measurements often are more convenient even when direct measurements would be possible.

Trigonometric ratios have other uses as well as those implied in the preceding paragraph. These occur in the sciences and their applications.

Trigonometry originated in efforts of the Greeks to study astronomy. Most of the advances in it were made by Hindu, Arabian, and European mathematical astronomers in their efforts to extend their knowledge of astronomy. In modern life, trigonometry is used by scientists and engineers.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN SURVEYING 

Women as well as men received training in surveying at the Monsanto Chemical Plant, St. Louis, during World War II.

Surveyors make the measurements and lay out the lines for such projects as mapping land, subdivision of a piece of land for real estate development purposes, construction of highways or railroads, water systems, oil pipe lines, and the construction of bridges, large structures, and even of small buildings, whan a maximum of accuracy of location, size, and level is desired. 

The principal tools of the surveyor are the transit, rods, of which at least one contains a target, steel tape, and markers such as those hanging from the belt of the young man in the picture. 

Training in surveying presupposes knowledge of trigonometry. It is usually secured by all civil engineers, and an introduction to it is part of the training of most of the students in colleges of engineering.

GM 

TRAIN OF TOMORROW 

263 

GENERAL MOTORS CORPORATION 

The railroad bed on which the "Train of Tomorrow" is shown was constructed only after a lot of work by surveyors.

The train itself is the product of a lot of mechanical and other industrial skill in which trained engineers, draftsmen, machinists, and many kinds of craftsmen cooperated.

Logarithms of Tangents

Tangents are used to solve certain problems. If you know how to use logarithms, then you are prepared to learn how to use the logarithms of tangents. These logarithms appear in the table on pages 363 to 367.

If you have not studied logarithms, omit this page. 

Thus: The logarithm of tan 32° is written log tan 32° and is often read log-tan-32.

On page 361, you find tan 32° = .6249. Therefore its logarithm must have the characteristic — 1, or 9 – 10. 

Log tan 32° is found on page 366 as follows: 

Find 32° in a column headed ANGLE. 

Opposite it, in the column headed LOG TAN, is 9.7958. Therefore log tan 32° = 9.7958 - 10. 

Note. "-10" is omitted in such tables. It is understood.

SEQUENCES OR PROGRESSIONS 

You know that debts often are paid by a sequence of payments. A problem of that kind appears on the opposite page. 

The monthly payments for a car or a refrigerator are probably the most familiar examples.

The payments are made in one, two, three order; namely, on a specified date each month.

There are many sources of sequences of numbers. You may have seen a pile of logs or of barrels, in layers, having one less log or barrel in each layer than in the one below it. 

You can imagine a growing town in which the population in each of four successive years has been 2000, 2400, 2880, 3456. If you study these numbers, you will find that each number is six fifths of the one preceding it.

In other words, there are several kinds of sequences. The numbers may all be alike; each number may differ from the preceding by a fixed number; each number may have a fixed ratio to the preceding. For any sequence, it is natural to inquire: 

What is some particular number of the sequence? 

What is the sum of all the numbers of the sequence?

In this chapter you will study three kinds of sequences, learn how to write specified terms of them, and how to find the sum of each.

MATHEMATICS IN USE IN COMPUTING MACHINES 

NEW ENGLAND MUTUAL LIFE INSURANCE COMPANY 

Computations for financial transactions as well as for science and industry often are long and involved, even when they are chiefly arithmetical. Such occur in the life insurance business, and in computing the payments of principal or interest on bonds. In both instances compound interest is useda subject studied in this chapter. 

To speed up such computing as well as to make it accurate, machines have been invented. A familiar example is the ordinary adding machine. There are electrically operated machines that will add, subtract, multiply and divide; others that will do various forms of business calculations and operations of higher mathematics. 

While operators can be trained to use such machines, someone with mathematical training must decide what it is that the machine shall be required to do-for the machine can do only what it is directed to do.

SOLVING DIFFICULT EQUATIONS 

GENERAL ELECTRIC 

Business and industry encounter many mathematical problems that cannot be solved by simple arithmetic. Some of their problems are so difficult that weeks of time would be required for trained persons to solve one of them. 

Many machines have been designed to do in a few minutes what men and women would require days to do. The machine shown here is one of these. 

Although it is remarkably efficient and accurate, yet one must not overlook the fact that its construction, upkeep, and operation demand the labor of many persons who know mathematics well — arithmetic, algebra, and other subjects.

A man contracted a debt of $400. He agreed to make twenty monthly payments as follows: 1 2 3 etc. Month 

Payment $22.00 $21.90 $21.80 $21.70 etc.

Each payment is 10¢ less than the preceding one. These payments form an arithmetic progression. 

Note. Accent arithmetic thus: ar-ith-met'-ic.

Rules and formulas by which to answer such questions as the following will be taught: 

What will his fifteenth payment be? 

What will the sum of all his payments be?

A sequence of numbers consists of numbers arranged in one, two, three order.

An arithmetic progression (A.P.) is a sequence of numbers, each of which after the first is derived from the preceding by adding to it or subtracting from it a fixed number. 

The terms of an arithmetic progression are the numbers of the progression.

The common difference of the progression is the difference between each term and the one preceding it. 285

Particular Term of an A.P.

Arithmetic Means 

Arithmetic means of two numbers are numbers between them that form an arithmetic progression with them. 

Thus: The three arithmetic means of 3 and 19 are 7, 11, and 

15 because 3, 7, 11, 15, 19 form an arithmetic progression.

The arithmetic mean of two numbers is the average of these numbers.

Geometric Progression 

Introduction. Suppose you save 2¢ this week, 4¢ next week, and continue, each week, to save twice as much as you did the previous week.

The savings form a geometric progression. You will learn how to write any term and the sum of any number of terms of such a progression.

A geometric progression (G.P.) is a sequence of numbers, called terms, each of which after the first is obtained from the preceding by multiplying it by a fixed number, called the ratio of the progression.

Thus: 16,4, +1, is a G.P. Its ratio is - because (−4) ÷ 16 = − 1; 1 ÷ (− 4) = − 1; (− 4) ÷ 1 − − 4. 

When the ratio is positive and more than 1, the terms form an increasing geometric progression.

When the ratio is positive and less than 1, the terms form a decreasing geometric progression. 

Thus: In 16, 8, 4, 2, the ratio is .

When the ratio is negative, the terms oscillate from plus to minus. 

Thus: In 3, -6, +12, − 24, ... ·, the ratio is 

2. The terms change from plus to minus and then from minus to plus. The terms do not decrease or increase steadily. 

In Examples 1 to 4: 

(a) Is the progression geometric? (b) If it is geometric, write the next three terms. 

1. 2, 6, 18, 54, 3. 10, 15, 20, ... 

2. 72, 24, 8,... 4.3, 

9, 27, +81, ... 

Write the first five terms of the G.P.

*Infinite Geometric Progression

If the number of terms of a sequence increases without limit, we say the sequence has an infinite number of terms and is an infinite sequence.

If the ratio of the G.P. is more than 1, the terms and the sum of them become infinitely large. 

But what happens when we have an infinite number of terms of a progression like 3, 1, 1, §, · · ·?

Evidently the terms become smaller and smaller, and, when the number of terms is infinitely large, the terms become approximately zero. 

If we stop at one of these very small terms, 312.

8-3- 12/12/424 1 

Since I is approximately zero, then is also.

3-0 .. S is approximately or 6. 1 - 1/2 This means that the sum of the progression is closer and closer to 6 as we increase the number of terms. 

Another way to see this is to write successive sums of the progression above. We get: 

S2 = 3 + 2, or 41; S4=54,

S3 

3+2+2, or 54 

54 +3, or 53; S5 = 5ğ +1³‰, or 51%

Notice that the sum is getting nearer and nearer to 6. 

Consider any geometric progression, a, ar, ar², ar³, etc. 

When r is a proper fraction, each term is less than the preceding. If n becomes infinitely large, arn becomes approximately zero. 

Therefore S = 

1 

arn becomes approximately α 0 1 

Therefore, the sum of an infinite number of terms of a geometric progression in which the ratio r is a proper fraction is given by the formula: S = 1 = 1

Powers of Binomials 

Recall that a binomial consists of two terms. In problems of compound interest and other applications of mathematics, we get results such as (a + x)², (a + x)³, (a + x)¹, etc. By long multiplication, we find: (a + x)² = a² + 2ax + x² 3 (a + x)³ a3+3a²x + 3ax² + x³ (1) (2) (3) (a + x)¹ = a¹ + 4a³x + 6a²x² + 4ɑx³ + x¹ The right side of each of these equations is said to be the expansion of the given indicated power of a + x. 

These expansions suggest the following rule:

Rule. To expand (a + x)" when n is a positive integer: (1) The first term is a". In each succeeding term the exponent of a decreases by 1 until it becomes 0. (2) The first term does not contain x. The exponent of x is 1 in the second term and increases by 1 in each succeeding term until it is n. (3) The coefficient of the first term is 1; of the second is n. (4) If any coefficient is multiplied by the exponent of a in that term and the product is divided by the number of the term, the quotient is the coefficient of the next term. 

REMARKS. 

1. The number of terms is n + 1. 

2. The sum of the exponents of a and x in each term is n. 

3. The coefficients of terms "equidistant" from the ends are the same. 

4. When x is negative, the terms are alternately plus and minus. a and x in these binomials may be any numbers or monomials. If "a," or "x," is a number like y², or - 2z, place it in parentheses before using the rules above. See Example 1, page 301.

The expansion obtained is a binomial series. If the series is given, it can be expressed as the power of a binomial in all elementary cases, and the sum of the series, then, is just the given power of the binomial.

EQUATIONS OF HIGHER 

DEGREE

In earlier chapters you have solved linear and quadratic equations having one unknown and a few equations of higher degree that are quadratic in form. In this chapter you will learn how to find integral or fractional roots of some equations of higher degree. 

A polynomial like 2x4 - 3x²+x-5 is a function of x because it has a value that can be computed when a value of x is given.

The symbol f(x) is read ƒ of x. It is used to represent some undefined function of x or is used to represent a given function that is under consideration. 

Thus: f(x) may represent 2x4 - 3x² + x − 5. 

In this case, f(− 2) = 2(− 2)¹ — 3 (− 2)² + (− 2) — 5 

A rational function of x is one in which x does not appear under a radical sign or with a fractional exponent. 

An integral function of x is one in which x does not appear in a denominator or with a negative exponent. 

Thus: 2x4 -3x² + x − 5 is rational and integral. 

Unless it is stated to the contrary, all functions discussed in this chapter are rational and integral, and have real coefficients; they are polynomials having one variable. 

The degree of a polynomial of one variable is the same as the exponent of its term having the highest exponent. 

Thus: 2x4x³ +3x-5 is of the fourth degree. 

1. If f(x) = x35x+8, what is: (a) f(2)? (b) f(-2)? 

2. If f(t) = at — gt2, find ƒ(2) when g = 32.2 and a = 100.

Remainder Theorem 

An expression can be factored by determining whether there is a remainder when the expression is divided by a possible factor.

EXAMPLE. IS x 2 a factor of x³ + x².

Subtraction of Integers and Decimals 

There are two methods of subtraction, the "take away" and the "additive." Use the method which you learned in the lower grade. Your teacher will help you to recall the method you are to use. 

Note to teacher. See the Teacher's Manual.

Division of Integers and Decimals 

1. Give the quotient and remainder when: 25 34 62 

(a) By 6 

57 46 73 68 

(b) By 5 (c) By 7 

49 58 are divided: (d) By 8 (e) By 9

Rule (a). When dividing a decimal by an integer, the number of places in the quotient is the same as the number in the dividend. Rule (b). When dividing a number by a decimal, change the divisor to an integer by moving its decimal point all the way to the right; then move the decimal point of the dividend the same number of places to the right. Then divide by Rule (a).

Problems to Follow Page 105 321 

1. Write an equation to express the fact that: (a) X varies jointly as y and z. (b) A varies jointly as the square of m and the cube of n. (c) C is directly proportional to x. (d) D varies directly as a² and inversely as b. (e) W varies jointly as r and s², and inversely as the cube of t. 

2. Using letters suggested by the statements, write an equation or formula to express: (a) The area of a sphere is directly proportional to the square of its radius. (b) The weight of an object above the earth is inversely proportional to the square of its distance from the center of the earth. (c) The volume of a given quantity of gas varies inversely as the pressure applied to it, if the temperature of the gas remains unchanged. 

3. Write the equation connecting the numbers in each part of this example. (a) S is directly proportional to r² and equals 45 when r = 3. (b) A varies inversely as x and equals 10 when x is 5. (c) B varies jointly as r and s, and is 20 when r is 5 and s is 12. (d) M varies jointly as c² and d, and is 20 when c is 4 and d is. (e) C varies directly as x2 and inversely as y²; also C is 11 when x = 5 and y 4. V 10. lwh. If I and h are constant, V is 600 when w = 15.

What is V: 

(a) When w = 5? (b) When w = 45? 

5. The electrical resistance of a wire varies jointly as its length and the square of its diameter.

If a wire 360 feet long and 3 mm. in diameter has a resistance of 1.08 ohms, how long is a wire of the same material having a resistance of 0.81 ohm, when the diameter is 5 mm.? 

6. The lift, L, of an airplane wing varies jointly as the coefficient of lift, C, the area of the wing in square feet, S, and the square of the velocity in feet per second, V. (a) Write the equation to express this law. (b) How then does the lift change when the area of the wing is increased 10%?

Problems to Follow Page 171 

325 1. A picture 15 in. wide and 20 in. long is to be surrounded by a frame whose area will be two thirds of the area of the picture. 

What must the width of the frame be? 

2. An open box is to be made from a square piece of tin by cutting out a 2 in. square from each corner and turning up the sides. How large must the original square be to make a box that contains 128 cu. in.? 

3. A new house was to be 28 feet wide and 32 feet long. It was found desirable to reduce its base area one quarter. The architect advised reducing the length 1 foot more than the width. 

Find the new dimensions, correct to tenths of a foot. 

4. The length of a rectangle is four times its width. Its area equals that of a square whose perimeter is 14 inches less than the perimeter of the rectangle. Find the dimensions of the rectangle. (Annapolis, 1938.) 

5. A number of coins can be arranged in a square having 6 coins on each side or in a rectangle having 5 more coins along its base than there are on its side. How many coins can be placed on the side and how many on the base of the rectangle? 

6. A, walking east at the rate of 3 miles an hour, passes a point 

P at the moment that B is 17 miles north of P. If B is walking south at the rate of 2 miles an hour, how long will it be until A and B are 15 miles apart in a straight line? 

7. The denominator of a fraction exceeds its numerator by 4. 

If 5 be subtracted from each, the sum of the reciprocal of the new fraction and four times the given fraction equals 5. Find the fraction. 

8. From a square piece of cardboard, 8 in. on a side, a box is to be made by cutting a square from each corner and folding the remaining cardboard to form a box with an open top. How large must the squares cut from the corners be in order that the box shall hold 9 times as much as a cubical box of which one face is the same as one of the squares cut out from the large square? 

9. A travels 224 miles in one hour less time than B. If A travels 4 miles per hour faster than B, find the rate at which each of them travels.

To Follow Page 185 ADDING COMPLEX NUMBERS GEOMETRICALLY 

Given: a + bi and c + di.

Construct (a + bi) + (c + di). 

CONSTRUCTION. 

1. Let M represent a + bi and N, (c+di). Draw OM and ON. 

2. From M, draw MP parallel to and equal to ON, in the direction of ON.

Statement. P represents (a + bi) + (c + di).

W T 1 RX 

PROOF. 1. Draw PR 1 OX. We must prove OR = (a + c) 

and PR = (b + d). Draw MS || OR, meeting PR at S. 

2. In AONW and AMSP: 

3. 4. 5. 6.

MP ON; OWN LMSP; LONW = LMPS 

AONWAMSP; PS = NW; MS = OW .. OR = OT + TROT + MS = OT+OW = a + c 

PR SR+PS MT+ PS = MT+NW = b + d 

P represents (a + c) + (b + d)i.

Note. In physics, it is proved that a force represented by OM and another represented by ON, both working on an object at 0, will have the same effect as a force represented by OP. OP is called the resultant of the two forces OM and ON.

Functions of the Form a(x - d) 2 

329 

EXAMPLE. Draw the graph of the function (x − 3)².

SOLUTION. 

1. Let y (x-3)2. 

When x 0 1 2 3 4 5 6 then y 9 4 1 0 149 

2. The graph of y = (x-3)2. 

is the same as that of y x2 but moved three units to the right. Its axis of symmetry is the +3; it has the line x = minimum value 0 when x = +3.

P X' ​x R X Y'

Rule. If xa be substituted for x in a function of x, the graph of the function is moved "a" units to the right when 'a" is positive, or to the left when "a" is negative. 

Thus: (x4)2 has the same graph as x2 but moved 4 units to the right, since a is 4. (x+4)2 has the same graph but moved 4 units to the left since x+4 = x − (− 4), and a is — 4.

Maximum and Minimum Problems 

331 

EXAMPLE. What must the length and width of a rectangular lot be to have a perimeter of 300 feet and have the greatest possible area?

SOLUTION. 

1. 2. 3. 4. (a) 

Let x the width and 150 x the length. 

Then the area is x(150 — x) square feet. 

For what value of x is x(150 - x) a maximum? 

Let y = x(150-x), or x² + 150x. 

(x² - 150x + 752) + 752 y (x − 75)² + 5625 (b) .. (c) .. (d) .. = - y has a maximum value of 5625 when x = 75. the width is 75 feet and the length is 75 feet.

20-2x

Problems to Follow Page 219 1. The distance from one corner of a rectangular field to the opposite corner is 20 yards; the distance between the two points along the intervening sides of the field is 28 yards. What are the dimensions of the field? 

2. The area of a lot at the corner where two streets meet is 

6000 square feet. A sidewalk 5 feet wide runs along the front of the lot and one side. The area of the sidewalk is 975 square feet. 

How long and wide is the lot? 

3. The area of the ground covered by a rectangular garden is 

450 square feet. It is divided into four equal parts by two paths, 3 ft. wide, parallel to its sides, passing through its center. If the area of the paths is 

126 square feet, what are the length and width of the garden? 

4. A dealer in fruits finds he can get 2 more boxes of oranges for $55 if he succeeds in buying them at 25¢ less than the asking price. What is the asking price, and how many boxes will he get at that price? 

5. A grocer paid $13.86 for a number of dozen eggs. An accident caused him to lose one dozen eggs. He sold the balance so that his margin on each dozen was 16¢, and his total margin was $4.70. How many dozen eggs did he buy? 

6. The difference of the squares of the tens' and units' digits of a two-digit number is 45. The number formed by interchanging the digits is three times the sum of the digits. What is the number? 

7. The sum of the terms of a fraction is 12. If the numerator be decreased by 2 and the denominator be increased by 1, the product of the resulting fraction and the given fraction is 7. Find the fraction if its terms are both positive. 

8. What are the lengths of the sides of the right triangle whose hypotenuse is 13 feet and whose area is 30 square feet? 

9. Find the number of two digits in which the units' digit exceeds the tens' digit by 3, and such that the product of the number and its tens' digit is 50. 

10. An airplane required hour longer to return than to fly 

400 miles. Its engine speed and the velocity of the wind remained unchanged throughout the trip. Its engine speed was of its flying rate on the return trip. What was the velocity of the wind?
